362 — Code Search Engine from Scratch

Project Number: 362

Project Name: Code Search Engine from Scratch

Owner: Mujir Qureshi

Document: FINAL_BLUEPRINT.md

Status: specification — locked

1. Goal

1.1 What Is Built

A trigram-indexed code search engine in Rust, tracing the lineage of Russ Cox's Google Code Search (2012). The system ingests large multi-repository codebases, extracts overlapping 3-byte trigrams from every file, accumulates immutable compressed posting-list shards, lowers an arbitrary regular expression into a boolean trigram query (sound over-approximation), intersects posting lists to a small candidate set, verifies each candidate with a linear-time Pike VM regex matcher producing exact line/column spans, ranks results, and serves substring / regex / symbol queries over HTTP/JSON with bounded latency and zero false negatives.

1.2 System Boundaries

Boundary Inside Outside
Ingestion local and remote git repos, filesystem trees live git fetch, OAuth flows
Query types literal, /regex/, file:, lang:, repo:, case:, sym: natural-language / semantic / vector search
Index format immutable shards, LSM compaction, manifest distributed index (multi-node sharding)
Matching line-level spans, symbol defs AST-level structural search (comby, semgrep)
Serving HTTP/JSON, streaming, single-host multi-shard Kubernetes ingress, auth, ACL, multi-tenant isolation
Regex engine Thompson NFA / Pike VM, RE2 syntax PCRE backreferences, lookahead/lookbehind
Language support extension + shebang + content-sniff detection full AST parse / type-checked symbol resolution

1.3 Explicit Non-Goals

  • No semantic (embedding) search; purely lexical trigram + regex.
  • No distributed index split across nodes; all shards on one host or shared filesystem.
  • No authentication, authorization, or multi-tenant access control.
  • No PCRE-style backtracking (by design; forbidden by P3 / I4).
  • No live file-system watch / inotify-driven hot-ingest; periodic or manual trigger only.
  • No web UI; HTTP/JSON API only.
  • No cross-language type inference or rename-aware symbol search.

1.4 Intended Deployment Model

Single binary (csearch-cli + csearch-server) on a Linux/macOS host with sufficient RAM to hold the active shard mmap windows. Typical deployment: a developer workstation or a CI machine indexing 1–50 repos (10 M – 500 M bytes of source). Production ceiling: a single-socket server with 16–128 GB RAM, NVMe storage, indexing hundreds of repos (up to ~50 GB source). The index directory is a local filesystem path; shards are memory-mapped at search time. csearch-server exposes port 7878 (configurable). No external dependencies beyond the Rust standard library and selected crates listed in Phase 1.

1.5 Target Users / Operators / Developers

Role Usage
Developer (user) csearch search 'fn parse_', csearch search '/impl\s+\w+/', csearch search 'sym:Parser'
Operator csearch index --repos /path/to/repos, csearch merge, csearch doctor
Integrator HTTP API consumer (IDE plugin, CI script, code review tool)
Portfolio engineer study trigram index design, regex lowering, shard format, incremental indexing

1.6 Language Rationale — Rust (Locked)

The hot path is trigram posting-list intersection then regex verification over memory-mapped index shards; it is a tight CPU+IO loop where varint posting decode, candidate-set intersection, and a linear-time regex matcher dominate. Rust gives mmap with safe lifetimes over the mapped bytes, zero-copy posting readers, SIMD-amenable decode, and exhaustive enums for the query AST and shard records, with no GC stalls during a multi-shard fan-out. The indexer, matcher, query planner, shard format, and search server are all Rust.

1.7 Phase 0 Deliverable — Locked Crates

The workspace compiles and all crates pass cargo test with property tests:

  1. csearch-core — DocId, RepoId, ShardId, Trigram, Posting, QueryAst, Filter, TrigramQuery, MatchSpan, SearchResult, RankScore, SymbolEntry, ShardHeader
  2. csearch-trigram — overlapping 3-byte extraction, dedup, frequency, case-fold
  3. csearch-regex — parse → Hir → Thompson NFA → Pike VM; linear-time guarantee
  4. csearch-trigquery — regex Hir → TrigramQuery; soundness obligation scaffolding
  5. csearch-index — postings accumulation, doc store, shard writer, varint+delta encode
  6. csearch-shard — mmap reader, posting-list decode, doc-meta table, header+CRC
  7. csearch-ingest — repo walk, language detection, binary/large-file filter, sha256
  8. csearch-symbol — ctags-style definition extraction, SymbolEntry posting index
  9. csearch-query — query language parser: literal, /regex/, filter fields
  10. csearch-search — executor: TrigramQuery → candidate DocIds → PikeVm verify → spans → rank
  11. csearch-incremental — change detection, per-repo shard rebuild, LSM compaction
  12. csearch-server — HTTP/JSON API, streaming, concurrent fan-out
  13. csearch-cli — index | search | merge | stat | doctor subcommands

1.8 Phase Unlocks

Phase Capability unlocked
Phase 1 Core types, trigram packing, property tests
Phase 2 Trigram extraction pipeline
Phase 3 Shard format: on-disk posting lists, mmap decode
Phase 4 Full index build from a repo tree
Phase 5 Linear-time PikeVm candidate verification
Phase 6 Regex → trigram query lowering (soundness)
Phase 7 Structured query language parser
Phase 8 End-to-end search executor
Phase 9 Production-quality ingestion (ignore, binary filter, sha)
Phase 10 Symbol index and sym: search
Phase 11 Ranking: density + path importance + symbol bonus
Phase 12 Incremental re-index, compaction, atomic manifest swap
Phase 13 HTTP/JSON server, streaming, concurrent multi-shard fan-out
Phase 14 Differential correctness (vs grep), fuzz, latency benchmarks

1.9 Deferred Features

  • PhaseDeferred("Phase 13") — multi-node distributed index
  • PhaseDeferred("Phase 13") — mTLS / JWT auth on HTTP API
  • PhaseDeferred("Phase 10") — full LSP symbol protocol (beyond ctags-style)
  • PhaseDeferred("Phase 14") — SIMD varint decode optimization
  • PhaseDeferred("Phase 14") — persistent query cache / result TTL cache

1.10 Timeline Estimate

Horizon Scope
Phase 0 (Phases 1–4): 6–14 weeks Core types, trigram, shard format, index build
Phases 5–8: +4–8 weeks Regex engine, lowering, query parser, search executor
Phases 9–11: +3–6 weeks Ingestion, symbol index, ranking
Phases 12–13: +4–8 weeks Incremental, server
Phase 14: +2–4 weeks Hardening, differential tests, benchmarks
Full system: 6–12 months All 14 phases to production quality

1.11 Prerequisites

  • Systems programming: memory layout, cache lines, page faults, mmap semantics
  • Compiler theory: NFA construction (Thompson), PikeVm / submatch semantics
  • Information retrieval: inverted index, posting lists, delta + varint encoding
  • File I/O: OS file-descriptor limits, mmap lifecycle, write barriers
  • Integer compression: varint (LEB128 / variable-length), gap encoding
  • Rust: lifetimes over mapped bytes, unsafe mmap slice coercion, bytemuck

2. Why Build This

2.1 Unique Systems Knowledge Taught

Topic Specific lesson
Trigram inverted index Why 3-grams balance false-positive rate vs index size; frequency distribution of English trigrams in code
Regex → boolean query lowering Converting a regex HIR to a sound TrigramQuery: AND across required substrings, OR across alternations, .* → All; soundness proof obligations
Thompson NFA + Pike VM O(n·m) regex matching; why backtracking is catastrophic; submatch capture via thread lists
Immutable shard design Write-once index files, CRC integrity seals, mmap lifetime safety, shard supersession
Delta + varint compression Gap encoding of ascending DocId lists; LEB128 / group-varint trade-offs; decoding cost vs compression ratio
Galloping intersection Advancing the shorter posting list with exponential probing; O(n log(m/n)) vs O(n+m) naive
LSM-style compaction Merging small shards into larger ones; maintaining the active manifest atomically; avoiding write amplification
Incremental indexing sha256 content addressing; detecting unchanged files; partial rebuild without global re-index
Linear-time regex guarantee Why O(n·m) matters for an adversarial public API; how to eliminate backtracking at the architecture level
Concurrent multi-shard fan-out Parallelizing searches across independent shard mmaps; deadline propagation; result-cap short-circuit

2.2 Historical and Architectural Significance

  • Google Code Search (2007–2012) was the reference public trigram-indexed code search engine; Russ Cox published the algorithms in two seminal essays (2007, 2012).
  • This design directly influenced Sourcegraph Zoekt (n-gram), Hound (trigram), and livegrep (trigram + RE2).
  • The candidate-then-verify pattern (P1) is a general IR principle: approximate index + exact verifier; it appears in full-text search (inverted index + snippet extract), database query planning (index scan + row filter), and packet classification.
  • The linear-time regex guarantee (P3) is the foundation of RE2 and Go's regexp package; it is a direct counter-example to "regex is fast enough" thinking in production systems.
  • Immutable shard + manifest swap (P4, I3, I7) mirrors LevelDB/RocksDB SSTable design at the file level; understanding it builds intuition for storage engines.

2.3 Engineering Muscles Developed

  • Boolean IR query planning (AND/OR posting-list algebra)
  • Finite automata theory applied to production-safe search
  • Binary file format design: magic bytes, version fields, CRC, backward compatibility
  • Unsafe Rust for mmap slice coercion with correct lifetime annotation
  • Property-based and differential testing (trigram roundtrip, soundness fuzz, grep diff)
  • Benchmark-driven optimization (posting decode, intersection, mmap read-ahead)
  • API design: streaming HTTP responses for large result sets

2.4 Production Engineering Concepts Exercised

Concept Where applied
Observability by default (P10) Per-query: candidate count, verified count, shards touched, stage latencies
Bounded latency (P6) Query deadline propagation across concurrent shard goroutines
Atomic manifest swap (I7) rename(2) or equivalent to atomically flip active shard set
Content addressing (I5) sha256 as document identity; deduplication across repos
Graceful degradation All TrigramQuery (no trigrams extracted) falls back to full-scan with warning
Fuzz testing Shard reader and trigram-query soundness; random regex inputs
Differential testing Phase 14: results must match grep -rE on same corpus

2.5 Cross-Links to Portfolio Projects

Related project Shared concept
Regex engine (from scratch) Thompson NFA, Pike VM, linear-time guarantee — csearch-regex is the production version
Compiler (lexer/parser) Regex HIR construction, EBNF grammar for query language (csearch-query)
Storage engine / LSM-tree Immutable shard + compaction mirrors SSTable lifecycle; manifest swap mirrors MANIFEST in LevelDB
Database query planner TrigramQuery lowering is a query rewrite rule: index predicate pushdown
Virtual machine PikeVm is a register machine with thread-list scheduling; parallels bytecode VM design
OS kernel / memory manager mmap lifecycle, page-fault cost, file-descriptor budgets
Content-addressable store sha256 content identity (I5) mirrors Git object store

2.6 Calibration Value

Building this system to production quality demonstrates: design of a domain-specific binary file format; formal correctness obligations (soundness over-approximation); safe Rust with raw memory; IR / NFA theory applied to a real system; IR compression and intersection algorithms; incremental and atomic update semantics; and end-to-end differential correctness testing. It is a portfolio anchor that covers compiler, storage, IR, and systems-networking concerns simultaneously.

2.7 What Mastery Looks Like

After building and studying this system end-to-end, the practitioner holds principal-engineer command over the following competencies, each earned in the named phase:

  • Trigram over-approximation reasoning (Phase 6): can derive a sound TrigramQuery from any regex Hir node by structural induction; can prove that a proposed lowering rule cannot produce false negatives; can calculate the worst-case candidate-to-verified ratio for a given query class.
  • Thompson NFA / Pike VM construction (Phase 5): can compile any RE2-subset regex to an NFA by hand, trace a Pike VM execution on a byte slice, prove O(|text|·|NFA|) by exhibiting the visited-bitset argument, and identify the exact code path that would need to change to introduce a backtracking bug.
  • Binary file format design (Phase 3): can design a write-once, mmap-safe binary layout with magic bytes, versioned header, CRC seal, and offset tables; can evolve the format across minor versions without breaking readers; can hand-decode a hex dump of a shard.
  • LSM compaction mechanics (Phase 12): can explain tiered vs. leveled compaction, derive the shard-count bound O(log N) under the merge policy, and write the atomic manifest swap using only POSIX rename(2); can identify the exact invariants that allow search to proceed concurrently with compaction without locks.
  • Galloping intersection and posting-list algebra (Phase 8): can implement galloping intersection from first principles, derive its O(n log(m/n)) complexity, explain when to switch to sequential merge, and apply the SvS (shortest-vs.-set) heuristic for ordering AND operands.
  • Unsafe Rust / mmap lifetime safety (Phase 3, 4): can write a PostingList<'a> type whose lifetime is tied to an Arc<MmapShard>, explain why the zero-copy contract holds, and audit any unsafe block in the shard reader for unsoundness.
  • End-to-end differential correctness (Phase 14): can design a differential harness that compares csearch output against grep -rE, identify the three root causes of false negatives (lowering bug, verification bug, candidate filtering bug), and write a minimal reproducer for each using the fuzz infrastructure.

3. Architectural Overview

3.1 Canonical Architecture Diagram

                        CODE SEARCH ENGINE  (index-once, search-many)
+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|   INDEX PATH                                                                        |
|   repos/ ---> csearch-ingest (walk, lang detect, filter, sha) ---> file stream      |
|                       |                                                             |
|                       v                                                             |
|   csearch-trigram (overlapping 3-byte trigrams)   csearch-symbol (ctags defs)       |
|                       |                                   |                         |
|                       v                                   v                         |
|   csearch-index (postings accumulate, doc store)  symbol postings                   |
|                       |                                                             |
|                       v   varint+delta encode                                       |
|   +------------------------------------------------------------------+              |
|   |  SHARD  { header | trigram table | posting lists | doc-meta }    |  (immutable) |
|   +------------------------------------------------------------------+              |
|                       ^  csearch-incremental: rebuild + merge (LSM compaction)       |
|                                                                                     |
|   SEARCH PATH                                                                       |
|   query string ---> csearch-query (parse: literal /regex/ file: lang: sym: case:)   |
|                       |  QueryAst                                                   |
|                       v                                                             |
|   csearch-regex (regex -> Hir -> NFA)   csearch-trigquery (regex -> TrigramQuery)   |
|                       |                            |                                |
|                       |                            v                                |
|                       |        csearch-shard.mmap: intersect posting lists          |
|                       |                            |  candidate DocIds              |
|                       v                            v                                |
|   csearch-search: VERIFY candidates with PikeVm -> MatchSpans -> csearch-rank        |
|                       |                                                             |
|                       v                                                             |
|   csearch-server (HTTP/JSON, streaming, multi-shard fan-out) ---> results            |
+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

3.2 Subsystem Descriptions

csearch-ingest (index path, entry). Walks a repo tree recursively; respects .gitignore / .ignore rules; detects language by extension + shebang + content sniff; skips binaries (null-byte scan), minified files (line-length heuristic), and files exceeding the size ceiling. Computes sha256 per file as content identity (I5). Emits a stream of (path, repo, lang, bytes, sha256) records to csearch-trigram and csearch-index. Trust boundary: untrusted filesystem content; enforces size + binary filters before any indexing work. No persistence of its own.

csearch-trigram (index path, trigram extraction). Receives raw file bytes; slides a 3-byte window one byte at a time (overlapping); deduplicates the per-document trigram set; optionally emits case-folded trigrams for case-insensitive mode; estimates trigram frequencies for cost planning. Output: Vec<Trigram> per document. Stateless; pure transformation. No I/O.

csearch-symbol (index path, symbol extraction). Lightweight per-language definition extraction using ctags-style regex heuristics (not full AST parse). Emits SymbolEntry { name, kind, doc_ref, line } records. Builds a parallel posting index keyed by symbol name (trigrams of the name for prefix/substring lookup, plus exact-match entries). Output fed into csearch-index alongside content trigrams.

csearch-index (index path, accumulation + shard writer). Accumulates (Trigram, DocId) pairs in memory; spills to temporary segments when memory ceiling is hit; sorts and merges segments; encodes posting lists as ascending delta+varint byte sequences. Captures DocMeta per document. Writes the final immutable shard file: ShardHeader, sorted trigram table (binary-searchable), concatenated posting list blobs, doc-meta table, trailing CRC. Assigns deterministic DocIds. Produces a build manifest. Persistence: write-once shard file.

SHARD (on-disk, immutable, mmap-able). Layout: { magic(4) | version(2) | ShardHeader(fixed) | trigram_table(sorted array of (Trigram, offset, len)) | posting_lists(blob) | doc_meta_table | crc32(4) }. Read via mmap; CRC verified on open. Never mutated after seal (I3). DocIds are local to the shard; globally unique as (ShardId, DocId).

csearch-shard (search path, mmap reader). Opens the shard file with mmap; verifies CRC; wraps a zero-copy PostingReader (seeks into the trigram table, returns a PostingList<'a> borrowing the mapped bytes). Decodes varint+delta on iteration. Provides doc_meta(DocId) -> DocMeta lookup. Concurrency boundary: multiple readers share the same mmap; writer creates a new file (never mutates). The mmap lifetime is tied to the ShardHandle.

csearch-incremental (index path, change detection + compaction). On re-index trigger: compares per-file sha256 + mtime against the previous manifest; rebuilds only repos with changed files into new shards; marks superseded shards in the manifest (I9). Runs LSM-style compaction: merges small shards into larger ones when shard count exceeds a threshold, producing a merged shard with filtered-out deleted docs. Atomically swaps the active manifest file (I7). Does not mutate existing shard bytes (I3).

csearch-query (search path, query parsing). Parses the user query string per the grammar: bare literal, /regex/, and filter fields (file:RE, lang:go, repo:RE, case:yes, sym:Name). Produces a QueryAst + Vec<Filter>. Reports structured errors for malformed input. Stateless; no I/O.

csearch-regex (search path, regex compiler + matcher). Parses a regex string into a Hir (high-level IR) using the regex-syntax crate. Compiles to a Thompson NFA. Executes via PikeVm (thread-list, leftmost-first, submatch capture). Guarantees O(len(text) × |NFA|) runtime — no backtracking (I4). Used by csearch-search to verify candidates and extract MatchSpans.

csearch-trigquery (search path, lowering). Walks the regex Hir to extract required substrings and alternations; converts each substring to an AND of its constituent trigrams; joins alternations with OR; emits a TrigramQuery tree. Soundness obligation (I1, P2): every document matching the regex satisfies the query. . / .* / short patterns → TrigramQuery::All (full-scan fallback). Tested by a fuzz harness in Phase 14.

csearch-search (search path, executor). Given a QueryAst + Vec<Filter>: lowers to a TrigramQuery via csearch-trigquery; fans out across all active shards (concurrent, per-query deadline P6); per shard: intersects/unions posting lists with galloping merge → candidate DocId set; applies Filter predicates on DocMeta (lang, repo, file-path regex); dispatches PikeVm verification per candidate; collects MatchSpans (line/col); calls csearch-rank; applies result cap; short-circuits. Emits per-query observability counters (P10).

csearch-rank (search path, scorer). Computes RankScore(f32) = match density (matches per line-count) + path importance (shallow path, non-test, non-vendor prefix) + symbol definition bonus + repo weight. Selects top-k via bounded max-heap. Tie-breaks deterministic by (path, line) (I10).

csearch-server (serving layer). HTTP/JSON API on configurable port. Endpoints: POST /search (query + filters + paging), GET /health, GET /metrics. Streams results as NDJSON. Maintains a ShardRegistry hot-reloaded on manifest swap (I7). Per-request deadline enforced. Concurrent requests limited by semaphore. Metrics: candidate_count, verified_count, shards_touched, stage_latency_ms.

csearch-cli (operator interface). Subcommands: index (build or re-index repos), search (interactive / scripted search), merge (explicit shard compaction), stat (shard statistics, trigram table sizes), doctor (CRC verification, manifest consistency check). Thin wrapper over the library crates.

4. System Constraints

4.1 Quantified Engineering Targets

Constraint Target Notes
Index memory ceiling (build) ≤ 2 GB RSS per shard build Spill to tmp segments when exceeded
Index memory ceiling (search) ≤ OS page cache; no explicit heap for posting lists Zero-copy mmap; only candidate set allocated
Candidate set heap (per query) ≤ 64 MB Bounded by result cap × shard count
Max source repo size ≤ 50 GB per index Beyond this, split into multiple index directories
Max file size (indexed) ≤ 2 MB per file Larger files skipped with warning; configurable
Max shard size ≤ 4 GB per shard file mmap safe on 64-bit; 32-bit not supported
Max shard count (active) ≤ 64 shards before compaction trigger Bounds fan-out concurrency
Max DocId per shard ≤ 2^32 (u32) ~4 B docs per shard; practical ceiling ~10 M
Trigram table lookup O(log N) binary search in mmap'd sorted array No hash table; sequential-friendly
Posting list intersection Galloping: O(n log(m/n)) n = shorter list; m = longer list
Regex compilation ≤ 10 ms per query regex Cached for repeated identical patterns
Pike VM verification O(len × NFA
p50 query latency ≤ 50 ms (10 M doc index, regex query, 8-core) Includes fan-out, intersection, top-20 verify
p95 query latency ≤ 200 ms Deadline propagated to all shard goroutines
p99 query latency ≤ 500 ms Result cap + deadline ensure hard upper bound
Query deadline Configurable; default 2 s Server returns partial results + timeout flag
Result cap Default 100 spans; configurable ≤ 10 000 Short-circuit verification after cap
Throughput (search) ≥ 20 concurrent queries on 8-core One thread per shard fan-out leg; async HTTP layer
Index build throughput ≥ 50 MB/s source ingestion (NVMe) Bottleneck: trigram extraction + posting sort
Incremental re-index ≤ 30 s for 1 changed repo (10 K files) Only changed repo re-indexed; others untouched
Shard open / CRC verify ≤ 500 ms per shard (4 GB) CRC via streaming read; mmap does not page-in
Startup to first query ≤ 2 s (64 shards, manifest load, mmap open) mmap is lazy; no preload required
Shutdown ≤ 500 ms Drain in-flight queries; close mmaps; no data loss
File descriptor budget ≤ 2 × shard_count + 64 overhead One fd per shard mmap + sockets
Storage amplification Index ≤ 30% of source byte size Trigram index + posting lists + doc-meta
Shard CRC collision budget CRC32 at 10^-9 false-pass rate Acceptable for integrity check; not cryptographic
CPU architecture x86_64 primary; aarch64 supported SIMD varint decode optional (Phase 14)
OS page size 4 KB assumed; huge-page optional mmap alignment to page boundary
Recovery objective (shard corruption) Detect on open (CRC fail) → exclude shard + alert No automatic repair; operator runs csearch doctor then rebuilds
Manifest consistency Atomic rename(2) swap; crash-safe In-progress build uses temp manifest; never partial-visible
Availability during re-index Search continues against old shards during rebuild New shard becomes visible only after manifest swap (I7)
Deployment scaling assumption Single host, NVMe or SSD, 16–128 GB RAM, 8–32 cores No network-distributed index in scope
Max concurrent HTTP connections ≤ 1 024 (configurable) Semaphore-bounded; excess → 503
Metrics scrape GET /metrics Prometheus text format; ≤ 5 ms Counters: queries, candidates, verified, errors, latency histograms

5. Required Reading

Reading priority legend: [P0] = required before Phase 1; [P1] = required before first use of that subsystem; [P2] = reference during implementation; [P3] = background / optional depth.

Recommended reading order for a new contributor:

  1. Cox 2007 (P2, Papers) — linear-time regex; understand the PikeVm before anything else.
  2. Cox 2012 (P1, Papers) — trigram index design; the candidate-then-verify architecture.
  3. Manning et al. B2 Ch. 1–2, 5 — boolean retrieval, postings, varint compression.
  4. Witten et al. B1 Ch. 3–4 — inverted index compression depth.
  5. google/codesearch R1 + R2 — canonical implementation of the above two papers.
  6. zoekt R3 + R4 — modern reference for shard format, ranking, and hot-reload.
  7. Remaining papers and books as needed per phase.

Books

# Title / Author(s) Chapters Concepts to Extract
B1 Managing Gigabytes — Witten, Moffat, Bell (2nd ed., 1999) [P0] Ch. 3 (inverted files), Ch. 4 (compression: unary, gamma, delta, Golomb), Ch. 5 (index construction) Delta+varint posting compression; merge-sort index build; blocked storage; gap/delta encoding for strictly-ascending DocId lists; memory-bounded build via runs. Applied in: §8.3/Phase 3 (posting encoding), Phase 4 (SPIMI-style memory-bounded build), Phase 8 (posting-list decode and intersection).
B2 Introduction to Information Retrieval — Manning, Raghavan, Schütze (2008, free online) [P0] Ch. 1–2 (boolean retrieval, postings merge), Ch. 5 (index compression: variable-byte, gamma), Ch. 11 (BM25 scoring), Ch. 4 (index construction: BSBI/SPIMI) Boolean AND/OR intersect algorithms; galloping (skip-pointer) intersect; variable-byte (varint) encoding; BM25 formula for RankScore; SPIMI bounded-memory build pattern. Applied in: Phase 4 (SPIMI-style spill+merge), Phase 8 (galloping intersection, P7/ADR-12), Phase 11 (BM25-derived match_density ranking, P9/ADR-12).
B3 The Art of Multiprocessor Programming — Herlihy & Shavit (2012) Ch. 9 (linked lists), Ch. 18 (skip lists), Ch. 3 (concurrent objects) Lock-free patterns for concurrent shard fan-out; safe mmap registry hot-reload; fence/ordering guarantees for manifest atomic swap (I7). Applied in: Phase 12 (manifest atomic swap, I7), Phase 13 (ArcSwap shard registry, concurrent fan-out).
B4 Compilers: Principles, Techniques, and Tools — Aho, Lam, Sethi, Ullman (Dragon Book, 2nd ed.) [P1] Ch. 3 (lexical analysis: NFA, DFA, subset construction), Ch. 3.7 (simulating NFA) Thompson construction reference; ε-closure; NFA state set simulation — foundation for csearch-regex Phase 5. Applied in: Phase 5 (Thompson NFA construction, ε-closure algorithm, I4).
B5 Regular Expression Matching Can Be Simple And Fast — Russ Cox (2007, online article) [P0] Entire article Pike VM mechanics; submatch capture with thread lists; why backtracking engines are O(2^n); how to implement linear-time NFA simulation in practice. Applied in: Phase 5 (Pike VM execution model, I4, ADR-08), §8.9 (NFA/PikeVm execution model).
B6 The Practice of Programming — Kernighan & Pike (1999) Ch. 9 (notation) Lightweight regex parser structure; simplicity-first NFA construction as model for csearch-regex. Applied in: Phase 5 (NFA state machine design philosophy, simplicity-first approach to csearch-regex parse.rs).
B7 Algorithms — Sedgewick & Wayne (4th ed.) Ch. 5.4 (regular expressions), Ch. 3 (symbol tables / hash maps) Digraph-reachability DFS for ε-closure; hash-map based trigram table lookups in shard. Applied in: Phase 5 (DFS ε-closure algorithm), Phase 3 (trigram table binary search in csearch-shard).
B8 Database Internals — Alex Petrov (2019) Ch. 6 (B-Tree variants), Ch. 7 (log-structured storage, LSM), Ch. 10 (MVCC) LSM compaction model for csearch-incremental Phase 12; manifest atomicity (I7); tiered vs. leveled compaction tradeoffs. Applied in: Phase 12 (LSM-style tiered compaction, CompactionPolicy, shard merge), §8.10 (incremental state transitions), P4/ADR-04.
B9 Systems Performance — Brendan Gregg (2nd ed., 2020) Ch. 7 (memory: mmap, page faults, TLB), Ch. 6 (CPUs: cache, prefetch) mmap warm-up cost; page-fault latency on cold shard access; NUMA effects on large mmap regions; profiling methodology for P5/P6. Applied in: P5 (mmap warm vs. cold latency, madvise hints), Phase 13 (MADV_SEQUENTIAL on fan-out, Performance Engineering section).
B10 Crafting Interpreters — Robert Nystrom (2021, free online) Ch. 16 (scanning), Ch. 30 (optimization) Illustrates hand-written lexer patterns applicable to csearch-query parser Phase 7; token error-recovery strategies. Applied in: Phase 7 (csearch-query lexer design, token error recovery in ParseError types).

Comput., 1977) Linear-time exact string search Baseline for literal-query fast path in csearch-search; confirms trigram over-approximation followed by exact string scan is optimal for literals. Applied in: Phase 8 (literal query fast path, verify.rs exact-string optimization), Phase 14 (differential correctness baseline with grep).

Applied in: Phase 3 (shard format design, P1 reference), Phase 6 (lowering from regexp.go), Phase 12 (merge algorithm from merge.go), §5 Comparative Architecture Table.

Applied in: Phase 12 (change-detection design contrast: sha+mtime over fsnotify), Comparative Architecture Table.
R6 livegrep (C++) src/codesearch.cc, src/tagsfile.cc, src/re_width.cc NFA fan-out across content; per-line byte offsets; real-world scale (kernel-sized repos) C++ memory model; RE2 integration is not a template for csearch-regex; livegrep's threading model predates async Rust. Applied in: Comparative Architecture Table (real-world scale reference), Phase 5 (per-line byte offset model from re_width.cc).

Applied in: Phase 5 (Hir type reference, ADR-10 regex-syntax adoption), Phase 6 (Hir variant enumeration in lower.rs).

RFCs / Specifications

# Reference Relevance
S1 RFC 3629 — UTF-8, a transformation format of ISO 10646 Byte-level properties of UTF-8: continuation bytes never match ASCII; trigram extraction over raw bytes (not codepoints) is correct and safe; boundary conditions for 2/3/4-byte sequences in Phase 2
S2 RFC 7159 / ECMA-404 — JSON data interchange format csearch-server HTTP/JSON response shape; wire format for SearchResult, DocMeta, MatchSpan serialization (Phase 13); serde_json usage
S3 RFC 7230 / RFC 9110 — HTTP/1.1 semantics + HTTP/2 HTTP server contract for csearch-server; chunked transfer encoding for streaming results; Connection: keep-alive; 4xx error codes for malformed queries
S4 POSIX.1-2017 — mmap(2), msync(2), munmap(2) mmap semantics: MAP_SHARED vs. MAP_PRIVATE; file-backed vs. anonymous; behavior on SIGBUS (file truncated); msync not needed for read-only mappings; platform divergence (macOS vs. Linux)
S5 SHA-256 (FIPS PUB 180-4) Content addressing in I5; DocMeta.sha256 field; sha2 crate correctness
S6 Varint encoding — Protocol Buffers encoding spec (Google, protobuf.dev/programming-guides/encoding/) Canonical definition of unsigned LEB128 / varint used in PostingWriter/PostingReader Phase 3; 7-bits-per-byte little-endian layout; max 5 bytes for u32
S7 .gitignore / .ignore pattern format (gitscm.com/docs/gitignore) csearch-ingest (Phase 9) must respect ignore rules; use ignore crate which implements the full gitignore spec including negation, double-star, anchoring
S8 Universal Ctags tag file format (ctags.sourceforge.net/FORMAT) SymbolEntry wire format; kind codes (f=function, c=class, m=member, v=variable, d=define); used by csearch-symbol Phase 10
S9 IEEE 754-2019 (binary32) RankScore(f32) arithmetic properties; NaN propagation if score formula divides by zero — must guard; total ordering for tie-break via f32::total_cmp
S10 Linux io_uring interface (io_uring.pdf, kernel.dk) Optional async I/O backend for csearch-ingest large-repo walk; not mandatory in Phase 9 but relevant if synchronous read throughput becomes the bottleneck

6. Design Principles

The twelve principles are organized into three groups: Correctness (P1–P3, P7, P11), Storage (P4–P5), and Operational (P6, P8–P10, P12). Each must hold end-to-end; violation of any correctness principle produces wrong results; violation of any operational principle produces unreliable service. Cross-references use Pn / In / Phase N notation.

Principles Summary

Principle Group Primary Crate(s) Key Invariant Violated By
P1 Candidate-then-verify Correctness csearch-trigquery, csearch-search I1, I6 Skipping PikeVm verification step
P2 No false negatives Correctness csearch-trigquery I1 Under-approximating lowering (returning fewer candidates than true matches)
P3 Linear-time matching Correctness csearch-regex I4 Using PCRE/backtracking engine in verify path
P4 Immutable shards Storage csearch-shard, csearch-incremental I3, I7 In-place mutation of shard bytes
P5 Zero-copy mmap Storage csearch-shard — Copying posting bytes to heap; unmapping while search in-flight
P6 Bounded latency Operational csearch-server, csearch-search — No deadline; unbounded fan-out; no result cap
P7 Correctness over size Correctness csearch-index I1, I2 Lossy compression (Bloom filters) for posting membership
P8 Deterministic results Operational csearch-rank I10 Timestamp/random in score; unstable sort
P9 Incremental rebuild Operational csearch-incremental I5, I9 Full-corpus rebuild on every file change
P10 Observability by default Operational csearch-server, csearch-search — Per-query telemetry behind a debug flag
P11 Fail-closed on malformed input Correctness csearch-query, csearch-shard, csearch-regex I3, I6 Best-effort coercion of malformed bytes into a default result instead of an explicit Err
P12 Bounded execution regardless of client input Operational csearch-server I12 Trusting a client-supplied (or absent) deadline with no server-side ceiling

P1. Candidate-then-Verify

Statement: the trigram index produces a candidate set — a superset of matching documents. A separate, exact regex matcher (PikeVm) verifies each candidate to produce precise MatchSpans. The index may over-approximate; the results must not.

Dimension Detail
Mechanism TrigramQuery evaluation (csearch-shard) narrows the universe from all DocIds to a candidate set; csearch-search::verify_candidate runs PikeVm over each candidate's raw bytes
Two-phase contract Phase A (index): produce candidate set ⊇ true matches; Phase B (verify): run PikeVm on each candidate; results = verified matches only
Tradeoff Larger over-approximation → more candidates → more I/O and CPU for verification; smaller over-approximation → fewer candidates → faster, but lowering is more complex
Operational implication Candidate count is always logged (P10 / I10). High candidate-to-result ratio signals a weak trigram query (e.g., very short pattern or .*) — surfaced in metrics, not treated as a bug
Key metric candidate_count / result_count ratio; ratio ≫ 1 on short literals triggers warning in QueryTrace
Violation consequence Skipping verification → false positives in results. Restricting candidates below true match set → false negatives, violating I1 and P2. Either breaks the correctness contract
Cross-references I1 (soundness), I6 (verification exactness), Phase 6 (lowering), Phase 8 (executor)

P2. No False Negatives

Statement: the regex-to-trigram lowering in csearch-trigquery MUST be a sound over-approximation. Every document matching the regex must satisfy the resulting TrigramQuery. Precision is an optimization; soundness is non-negotiable.

Dimension Detail
Formal obligation For all documents D, regex R: R matches D ⟹ D satisfies lower(R). The converse need not hold.
Implementation anchor .* lowers to All; alternation `a
Hir node mapping (see table) Literal → trigrams; Concat → And; Alternation → Or; Repetition(*) → All; Class → All
Tradeoff Sound lowering of pathological regexes (e.g., `(a
Precision optimization Within the And tree, prefer trigrams with the highest corpus selectivity (lowest posting-list length) — computed via frequency table from Phase 2; does not affect soundness
Violation consequence Under-approximation (too few candidates) → a document that matches the regex is never verified → that match is silently lost from results; undetectable without differential testing
Testing Fuzz: for random regex R and random corpus, assert true_matches(R) ⊆ candidates(lower(R)); any counterexample is a soundness bug. Phase 14 differential test vs. grep -rE enforces this at corpus scale
Cross-references I1 (invariant statement), Phase 6 (lowering implementation), Phase 14 (fuzz harness)

Lowering rules — complete mapping of Hir nodes to TrigramQuery:

Hir Node TrigramQuery Result Notes
Literal(bytes) where len ≥ 3 And of all overlapping 3-byte trigrams in bytes Extract trigrams: (b[0],b[1],b[2]), (b[1],b[2],b[3]), …
Literal(bytes) where len < 3 All Cannot commit to any trigram
Concat([a, b, c, …]) And([lower(a), lower(b), lower(c), …]) Preserve only non-All children
Alternation([a, b, …]) Or([lower(a), lower(b), …]) If any child is All, whole Or is All
Repetition(e, {0,}) i.e. e* All Zero repetitions match empty string — no trigram guaranteed
Repetition(e, {1,}) i.e. e+ lower(e) At least one occurrence guarantees e's trigrams
Repetition(e, {n,}) n ≥ 1 lower(e) Same logic
Class(_) All Character class with multiple values → no specific trigram guaranteed
Dot (any byte) All Cannot commit
Anchor All Pure positional; no bytes consumed
Capture(e) lower(e) Transparent; forwarded to inner expression
Look(_) (lookahead) All Zero-width; rejected at parse time for verify; treated as All for lowering

P3. Linear-Time Matching Only

Statement: the verification engine in csearch-regex is a Thompson NFA compiled to a Pike VM. It runs in O(n × m) time (n = text length, m = NFA size). Backtracking is forbidden.

Dimension Detail
Mechanism Pike VM maintains a set of active NFA threads (no stack); each input byte advances all threads simultaneously; catastrophic backtracking is structurally impossible
NFA thread set At each input position, active NFA states ≤ size(NFA); duplicates deduplicated; no state visited twice at same position
Compilation pipeline query_string → regex-syntax Hir → Thompson NFA (states + ε-transitions) → PikeVm thread-list execution; no DFA construction required
Unsupported constructs Backreferences \1, lookahead (?=...), lookbehind (?<=...) — rejected at parse time with a descriptive error; PCRE-style possessive quantifiers also rejected
Tradeoff Pike VM cannot implement backreferences; symbol search (Phase 10) that needs backrefs falls back to simpler heuristic pattern, not regex verify
Operational implication A malicious query with a pathological pattern (e.g., (a+)+) cannot hang csearch-server; latency is bounded by corpus size × NFA size, not input content
Violation consequence Any backtracking engine substituted here becomes a server DoS vector; a single adversarial document + query could consume unbounded CPU
Cross-references I4 (linear-time invariant), Phase 5 (NFA/PikeVm implementation), Phase 14 (adversarial fuzz)

P4. Immutable Shards, Merge to Compact

Statement: a shard is written once, CRC-sealed, and never mutated. Updates produce new shards. An LSM-style merge (csearch-incremental) bounds shard count over time.

Dimension Detail
Mechanism ShardHeader includes magic + version + CRC; any write or truncation is detectable at open time; csearch-shard reader rejects mismatched CRC
Shard lifecycle Write → seal (CRC) → register in manifest → serve queries → supersede (incremental) → compact (merge) → unregister
On-disk structure [ShardHeader][trigram_table: sorted Trigram→offset pairs][posting_lists: varint+delta blobs][doc_meta_table: fixed-width DocMeta records]
Compaction trigger Shard count exceeds configurable threshold (default: 16); triggered by csearch-incremental after each repo rebuild; merge writes a new combined shard then atomically swaps manifest
Tradeoff More shards → more fan-out per query (linear in shard count); compaction reduces fan-out at the cost of I/O during merge; similar to RocksDB L0 flush vs. compaction
Operational implication Manifest records shard set; atomic manifest swap (I7) means readers see consistent sets; deleted docs accumulate until compaction (I3/I9)
Violation consequence In-place mutation of a shard while a reader has it mmap'd → data race; corrupts MatchSpans; undetectable by CRC at read time unless re-checked
Cross-references I3 (shard immutability), I7 (manifest atomicity), I9 (deletion via supersession), Phase 3 (shard format), Phase 12 (incremental+merge)

P5. Zero-Copy Reads via mmap

Statement: posting lists and doc-meta records are read from memory-mapped shard bytes. The search hot path allocates only the candidate set, the result list, and line-text slices.

Dimension Detail
Mechanism PostingList<'a> borrows directly from the mmap region; DocMeta fields are parsed from fixed-offset mmap slices; no intermediate heap allocation per posting
Lifetime contract mmap region lives in Arc<MmapShard>; search task holds clone; unmap deferred until all Arc clones drop
PostingList<'a> Lifetime 'a is tied to the Arc<MmapShard> borrow; the compiler enforces that the posting list does not outlive the shard
Cold vs. warm Cold shard (not in OS page cache): page-fault per 4 KB page on first access; warm shard: near-memory-bandwidth reads; madvise(MADV_SEQUENTIAL) hint on posting-list regions improves prefetch
Tradeoff Cold-start page-fault latency on first access; large shard sets consume virtual address space (manageable on 64-bit); mmap file must remain open while any search is in flight
Platform note Linux: MAP_POPULATE can pre-fault on warm-up; macOS: no MAP_POPULATE, use sequential read pre-warm; memmap2 crate abstracts POSIX mmap on both
Operational implication Warm index (pages in OS page cache) gives read throughput approaching memory bandwidth; hot reload (I7) must not unmap pages still referenced by in-flight searches — use Arc-ref-counted shard handles
Violation consequence Copying each posting to heap → 2–5× memory bandwidth; prohibitive at scale; also invalidates the latency budget of P6
Cross-references P6 (latency), I3 (shard immutability enables safe concurrent mmap), Phase 3 (shard layout for zero-copy decode)

P6. Bounded Query Latency

Statement: every query carries a per-query deadline and a result cap. Fan-out across shards is concurrent; each shard worker short-circuits when the cap is met or the deadline expires.

Dimension Detail
Mechanism tokio::time::timeout wraps each shard task; AtomicUsize result counter shared across shard tasks; tasks check counter and deadline before verifying each candidate
Fan-out model Each shard spawned as a tokio task; results streamed into a shared channel; coordinator collects until result_cap or deadline fires, then cancels remaining tasks via CancellationToken
Defaults result_cap = 1000; deadline = 5 s; both per-request overridable via HTTP query params; hard max result_cap = 10 000
Tradeoff Early exit → results from the first N matching shards (by schedule order); result set is not globally optimal under ranking; acceptable because ranking re-sorts within the cap
Operational implication Per-query telemetry includes shards_hit, candidates_scanned, results_returned, deadline_hit flag (P10/I10); deadline_hit=true is a signal to operator that the query needs a more selective trigram query
Violation consequence Unbounded fan-out on a slow shard holds the HTTP response open indefinitely; at high concurrency, causes thread/task exhaustion and request queue buildup
Cross-references P10 (observability), P5 (mmap latency), Phase 13 (server fan-out impl), I7 (shard set snapshot per query)

P7. Index Correctness Over Index Size

Statement: the compression scheme for posting lists is lossless. No approximation (e.g., Bloom filter, sketch) replaces exact trigram posting membership. A smaller, lossy index is unacceptable.

Dimension Detail
Mechanism delta+varint encoding is mathematically lossless; decode(encode(list)) == list is a property test in Phase 3
Why Bloom filters are wrong here A Bloom filter for a posting list has false-positive rate ε > 0; any false positive in a posting list means a document is excluded from the candidate set for that trigram — that document may be a true match → false negative in results
Lossless compression options delta+varint (chosen: simple, fast decode, good ratio for sequential IDs); PFOR-delta (better ratio, more complex decode — evaluate in Phase 14 if size is a bottleneck)
Tradeoff Lossy structures (Bloom filter in place of posting list) would reduce I/O at the cost of false negatives — which violates I1/P2
Operational implication Index size is a secondary metric after correctness; compression ratio is monitored but never traded against soundness
Size levers Binary/minified file exclusion (Phase 9); document size cap; trigram frequency cap (ultra-common trigrams like th posted to millions of docs — consider omitting high-frequency trigrams and defaulting their queries to All)
Violation consequence A lossy posting structure silently omits DocIds → missing candidates → false negatives in search results; no user-visible error, no CRC failure
Cross-references I1 (soundness), I2 (posting order), I8 (trigram bijection), Phase 3 (encoding), Phase 7 (correctness)

P8. Deterministic Results

Statement: given a fixed index state and an identical query, csearch-search returns the same ordered results on every invocation, across restarts and replicas.

Dimension Detail
Mechanism RankScore tie-break is total: primary = score descending, secondary = (shard_id, doc_id) ascending; heap selection is stable; no random or time-dependent terms in score formula
f32 ordering Use f32::total_cmp for score comparison; avoids NaN-induced non-determinism; guard against division-by-zero in score formula
Tradeoff Determinism constrains the ranking formula (no temperature/random sampling); also means rank order can be unit-tested with golden fixtures
Operational implication Regression tests compare exact result order against stored golden outputs; any score formula change is a breaking change requiring golden update
Fan-out ordering Shards are assigned stable IDs at manifest registration; fan-out task scheduling is non-deterministic, but results are merged and re-sorted before returning — scheduling order does not affect final output
Violation consequence Non-deterministic results confuse users; breaks integration tests; impossible to reproduce bug reports
Cross-references I10 (determinism invariant), Phase 11 (ranking), Phase 14 (golden test harness)

P9. Incremental Over Full Rebuild

Statement: re-indexing after a repository change rebuilds only the affected repo's shard. A single file change never triggers a global rebuild.

Dimension Detail
Mechanism csearch-incremental computes a change set: files where sha256 or mtime differs from the prior manifest record; only those repos are re-ingested; the rebuilt shard replaces the old one in the manifest
Change detection Two-phase: (1) mtime compare (cheap); if changed: (2) sha256 (confirms real content change vs. touch); only sha256-changed files re-ingested
State machine Unchanged → no action; Modified → re-ingest file; Added → ingest new; Deleted → mark superseded in manifest; Renamed → Deleted + Added
Granularity Per-repo: a single changed file causes the entire repo shard to be rebuilt; acceptable because repo shards are small (one repo); does not scale if a single git monorepo contains millions of files
Tradeoff Per-repo granularity means a monorepo with frequent changes re-indexes the full monorepo shard; sub-repo granularity (per-directory shards) is a future optimization
Operational implication Background incremental sweep on configurable interval (e.g., 60 s); operator can force full rebuild via csearch-cli index --force; csearch-cli stat shows per-repo last-indexed timestamp
Violation consequence Full rebuild on every change → index latency proportional to total corpus size; unusable for large repositories with frequent commits
Cross-references I5 (content addressing), I7 (manifest atomicity), I9 (deletion), Phase 9 (ingest), Phase 12 (incremental impl)

P10. Observability by Default

Statement: every query emits structured telemetry: candidate count, verified count, shards touched, per-stage latency, deadline-hit flag, result count. No optional/debug-only flag required.

Dimension Detail
Mechanism QueryTrace struct populated during csearch-search execution; emitted via tracing::info! spans with structured fields; csearch-server exports as JSON at /metrics; Prometheus-compatible counters
QueryTrace fields query_text, trigram_query_type, shards_fanned_out, candidates_total, candidates_verified, results_returned, deadline_hit, stage_latency_ns[parse,lower,intersect,verify,rank]
Slow-query threshold If stage_latency_ns[verify] > 2 s, emit WARN log with full QueryTrace; operator action: examine trigram query selectivity
Index-level metrics index_shard_count, index_doc_count, index_size_bytes, last_incremental_duration_ms, uncompacted_deleted_docs — exposed via /metrics
Tradeoff Trace allocation per query adds ~100 ns overhead; acceptable given latency budget of seconds; QueryTrace uses stack allocation where possible
Operational implication Operators can diagnose slow queries (high candidate count → weak trigram query), index drift (shard count growing), and verification bottlenecks (high verify:candidate ratio) without instrumenting code
Violation consequence Without per-query telemetry, diagnosing correctness regressions (e.g., false-negative rate change) requires corpus-level differential testing; operational blind spot
Cross-references I10 (determinism), P6 (bounded latency with deadline_hit), Phase 13 (server metrics endpoint)

P11. Fail-Closed on Malformed or Ambiguous Input

Statement: any malformed shard byte, unparsable query string, or unsupported regex construct produces an explicit, typed error at the earliest boundary — never a silently coerced result, a panic, or undefined behavior.

Dimension Detail
Mechanism Parse-boundary validation in csearch-query (reject null bytes, cap query length at 4096 B); CRC + magic check in csearch-shard before any decode; csearch-regex rejects backreferences/lookaround at parse time rather than approximating them
Enforcement points csearch-query::parse (400 on malformed query), csearch-shard::open (Err on CRC/magic mismatch), csearch-regex::compile (Err on unsupported construct)
Tradeoff Rejecting early sacrifices leniency — the engine cannot "best-effort" recover a malformed query or a slightly-corrupt shard — in exchange for auditable, fuzzable correctness boundaries
Operational implication Every rejection is logged with the offending input class (truncated preview, never full sensitive bytes) and surfaced as a 4xx client error, never a 5xx
Violation consequence Silently coercing malformed input (e.g., truncating a bad regex node to a permissive default, or decoding a corrupt shard byte as zero) turns a client-visible bug into a server-side correctness defect indistinguishable from a real soundness bug
Cross-references T3 (malformed shard), T13 (HTTP query injection), F4 (posting-list decode panic), I3 (shard immutability/CRC), Phase 3 §3.6, Phase 7 §7.6 pitfalls

P12. Bounded Execution Regardless of Client Input

Statement: the server enforces a hard execution ceiling on every query independent of any client-supplied deadline; no query may hold a worker thread or HTTP connection open indefinitely.

Dimension Detail
Mechanism csearch-server computes effective_deadline = min(client_deadline, hard_ceiling) with hard_ceiling defaulting to 30 s; CancellationToken fires fan-out cancellation at whichever bound is smaller
Default hard_ceiling = 30 s, operator-configurable; applies even when a client omits deadline_ms entirely
Tradeoff A legitimate but pathological query (e.g., unbounded .* scan on a huge corpus) is truncated rather than run to completion — favors server availability over completing every query
Operational implication hard_ceiling_hit_total metric flags when clients are relying on unbounded queries, signaling a need for narrower trigram queries, added filters, or corpus partitioning
Violation consequence Without a server-enforced ceiling, a client bug or adversarial request exhausts the Tokio worker pool, producing the server-crash-under-concurrency failure mode (F13)
Cross-references P6 (the client-facing bounded-latency contract this backstops), I12 (the enforceable invariant this principle motivates), Phase 13 13.9 failure analysis (runaway query)

7. System Invariants

Invariants are categorized by enforcement layer: [BUILD] = guaranteed by construction at index-build time; [RUNTIME] = enforced at query runtime; [TEST] = validated by tests only; [STORAGE] = guaranteed by on-disk format.

Inv Statement Why It Matters Enforced In Detection Telemetry Recovery Debug Method
I1 For all D, R: R matches D ⟹ D ∈ candidates(lower(R)) (no false negatives) [BUILD] Foundation of correctness; a missed candidate means a missed result with no error signal csearch-trigquery: every lowering path must produce All when in doubt Phase 14 fuzz: random regex × corpus; differential vs. grep -rE; any counterexample is a critical bug candidates_returned; per-query trigram_query_type=all flag when lower produces All No runtime recovery; if regression found: re-examine lowering for the triggering regex class; add property test csearch-cli search --explain dumps TrigramQuery; compare candidate set vs. grep output on a small corpus
I2 Every posting list is strictly ascending by DocId (no duplicates, no out-of-order entries) [BUILD] Enables galloping intersection; correctness of AND/OR set operations depends on monotone order csearch-index: PostingWriter sorts and deduplicates before write; csearch-shard: debug-build assertion in PostingReader Debug-build assertion in PostingReader iterator; integration test: decode any shard list and assert strictly increasing N/A (structural invariant) Shard is malformed; reject at CRC/validation; force rebuild csearch-cli stat --shard <path> --check-posting-order scans all posting lists
I3 Once a shard is CRC-sealed and written, its bytes never change; supersession recorded in manifest only [STORAGE] Enables safe concurrent mmap reads without locking; CRC detects disk errors or accidental mutation csearch-shard: reader opens file O_RDONLY; csearch-incremental: always writes new path, never in-place CRC check at shard open; csearch-cli doctor periodic fsck shard_crc_failures_total Re-build the corrupted shard; mark invalid in manifest csearch-cli doctor --shard <path>; compare file mtime vs. manifest write timestamp
I4 PikeVm runs in O(n × m): n = input bytes, m = NFA state count; no super-linear blowup [RUNTIME] Prevents server DoS from adversarial queries; latency predictable csearch-regex: thread-set deduplicates NFA states; at-most one visit per (state, position) pair Fuzz with adversarial patterns ((a+)+); measure wall time vs. input length; super-linear growth = bug verify_ns_per_byte per query Reject backreferences at parse time; unsupported constructs → error Compile regex, print NFA size; run on 1 KB / 10 KB / 100 KB; assert linear scaling
I5 A document's identity is its sha256 digest; re-indexing identical content is a no-op [BUILD] Prevents redundant work on incremental rebuild; enables dedup detection csearch-ingest: sha256 before file stream; csearch-incremental: sha256 comparison in change-set Unit test: index same file twice; assert DocId stable, shard unchanged files_skipped_unchanged_total None needed; sha256 mismatch always triggers rebuild Dump DocMeta before and after no-op rebuild; assert sha256 field identical
I6 Every MatchSpan is an actual regex match at exact byte offset, line, and column [RUNTIME] User-visible correctness: highlighted span is the real match csearch-search: PikeVm capture group 0 → byte bounds; line/col by newline scan in doc_bytes[..offset] Golden tests: fixed corpus + queries → fixed spans; differential vs. grep -n spans_returned per query No runtime recovery; wrong span = PikeVm capture bug Extract doc bytes from shard; run PikeVm locally; compare spans to grep -obn
I7 Active shard set is swapped atomically via manifest; readers see old or new set, never a partial one [STORAGE] Prevents mixed-manifest queries with duplicates or missing docs csearch-incremental: write .tmp then rename(2) (atomic POSIX); csearch-server: manifest reload under RwLock Assert manifest valid before rename; integration test: concurrent queries during index swap manifest_reloads_total, manifest_reload_errors_total If rename fails, old manifest stays active; operator retries Inspect manifest mtime; check for .tmp leftovers; csearch-cli stat --manifest
I8 Trigram pack/unpack is a bijection: unpack(pack(b0,b1,b2)) == (b0,b1,b2) for all byte triples [BUILD] Entire index correctness depends on unique trigram keys; collision merges unrelated posting lists csearch-core: pure functions Trigram::pack / unpack; property test Phase 1 PropTest: sample u8³ space, assert roundtrip and injectivity N/A (compile-time / test-time) No runtime recovery if property test passes Run proptest suite; verify bit layout `b0<<16
I9 A deleted doc is filtered at query time and produces no result, until compacted [RUNTIME] Ensures stale files do not pollute results after incremental update csearch-search: check manifest deletion set before verifying each candidate; csearch-incremental: appends superseded (ShardId, DocId) to deletion set Integration test: index → delete → rebuild → assert absent; differential vs. filesystem deleted_docs_filtered_total per query If deletion set lost: worst case stale results (not data loss); rebuild manifest csearch-cli stat --deleted; csearch-cli merge compacts away deleted docs
I10 Identical index + identical query → identical ordered results, every run [RUNTIME] Reproducible results; testable ranking; golden fixture stability csearch-rank: tie-break (RankScore desc, ShardId asc, DocId asc) via f32::total_cmp; no timestamp/random in score Golden fixture tests: serialize result list as JSON; any diff = intentional change N/A (structural property) Non-determinism = ranking bug; fix at source Run same query twice with --explain; diff JSON; trace divergence to score formula or sort instability
I11 Within a shard, TrigramTable entries are sorted ascending by Trigram value with no duplicates [BUILD] Enables O(log n_trigrams) binary-search lookup; an unsorted table silently returns wrong or missing posting lists with no error csearch-index: PostingWriter::finish sorts before write; csearch-shard: write_shard asserts sortedness in debug builds Debug-build assertion in write_shard; integration test scans TrigramTable and asserts strict ascending order shard_build_sort_assert_total (debug builds only) Reject the build; fix the PostingWriter::finish contract; rebuild the shard csearch-cli doctor --shard <path> --check-trigram-order scans table order
I12 Every query terminates within min(client_deadline, hard_ceiling=30s); no query blocks a server worker indefinitely [RUNTIME] Prevents a single pathological or misconfigured query from exhausting worker threads or holding an HTTP connection open forever (P12, F13) csearch-search: executor deadline check (P6); csearch-server: hard ceiling applied even when no client deadline is supplied Integration test: submit a query with no deadline on a pathological corpus; assert response within hard_ceiling + small margin deadline_hit flag; hard_ceiling_hit_total counter Response returns partial/truncated results (truncated=true); no operator action needed unless hard_ceiling_hit_total trends up Reproduce with csearch-cli search --deadline none; inspect wall-clock via QueryTrace.elapsed_µs_by_stage

Invariant Enforcement Matrix

The table below maps each invariant to the crate(s) that own its enforcement and the test type that validates it.

Invariant Primary Crate Secondary Crate Test Type Phase Introduced
